<p>Do not label any of these four as Boerum. Do not silently assign the Porter photography credit to Johnson.</p>
<h2 id="research-offices/boerum-street-reference"><a class="section-anchor" href="#research-offices/boerum-street-reference">Boerum street reference</a></h2>
<p>The supplied identification associates the three adjoining frontages with the Boerum offices. The street study keeps them joined along the sidewalk. Brick, pale glazing grids and the tree create a different street character from Porter’s charcoal frontage.</p>
<p><a href="assets/office-references/boerum-user-street-view.png">Open the supplied street view</a>. User-supplied screenshot of Google street imagery; capture date not established. Use as a local review reference. The screenshot itself is not a game texture.</p>
<p>The anonymous fedora-clad pedestrian follows a six-leg, 36-second loop with two moonwalking legs. This is ambient motion, not a source claim about a person’s actions or knowledge. Pause, reduced-motion and hidden-scene behavior are implemented. The <a href="assets/sprite-catalogue/sheets/boerum-animation-labelled.png">labelled animation sheet</a> shows the distinct walking, moonwalking and endpoint poses.</p>
<h2 id="research-offices/what-the-component-kit-takes-from-the-photographs"><a class="section-anchor" href="#research-offices/what-the-component-kit-takes-from-the-photographs">What the component kit takes from the photographs</a></h2>
<p>These are proposed game translations, not measured reconstructions. Full footprints and roof plans have not been established.</p>
<div class="table-scroll" tabindex="0" role="region" aria-label="Scrollable specification table"><table>
<thead>
<tr>
<th>Kit element</th>
<th>Current translation and retained design rule</th>
<th>Source basis / limit</th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody><tr>
<td>Industrial shell</td>
<td>A long, low-parapet masonry volume assembled from repeated bays. Vary bay widths and glass heights; use an inset entrance and a narrow dark shadow at the lintel. Make it materially substantial enough to support a tall internal hall.</td>
<td>Porter exterior. The exact roof shape behind the parapet is not established by the frontal photo.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Roof family</td>
<td>Broad mineral-grey roof planes, parapet returns and low raised skylight units. Roof removal exposes the interior. Utility details remain grouped.</td>
<td>Skylights and exposed roof members are directly visible in both office sets. Skylight positions in the sprite are a compositional proposal.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Window kit</td>
<td>Two tall industrial grids, one broad black meeting-room window and a glazed double entrance. A 1-pixel dark mullion, brighter outer reveal and restrained glass ramp will do more than a repeated flat blue square.</td>
<td>Porter exterior, Johnson mezzanine and Porter room-boxes.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Interior insert</td>
<td>Pale timber box with vertical battens, a black-framed window and a deeply coloured doorway recess. It sits below the shell roof, with a visible gap above. Make two lengths from the same bay.</td>
<td>Porter working hall and close view linked below.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Vertical circulation</td>
<td>Thin orange stair stringers and guardrail, grey tread tops, dark gaps between risers. This can be a reusable stair/landing unit with a clear silhouette at normal zoom.</td>
<td>Johnson mezzanine. Preserve its orange material identity even if the operating department changes.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Workstation</td>
<td>White slab with a warm ply edge, charcoal screen, coloured supports and a separate chair. Rows use repeated stations with sparse personal props. Storage/divider variations remain possible additions.</td>
<td>Porter working hall and Johnson desks. The current white-worktable component is M-01.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Social module</td>
<td>A rectangular coral booth with a pale tall divider; a curved or straight planter-seat edge; pale round tables with blue supports. Use one dominant shape per module.</td>
<td>Porter gathering area. Booth shape survives reduction better than many miniature accessories.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Interior landmark</td>
<td>Johnson’s cutaway includes a central white birch, connected orange stair and office cat; the separate Solarium is an invented Porter-derived glass attraction. The Chrysler can be parked or follow its invented circuit.</td>
<td>Both office sets show a white car; Johnson shows the Chrysler and central tree clearly. Similar appearance does not establish a relocation history.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Street edge</td>
<td>Concrete sidewalk adjoining the facade, asphalt/service paving, a hydrant or bollards, occasional tree pits. Retain planted pockets as deliberate places rather than a continuous lawn apron around every building.</td>
<td>Porter exterior; placement beyond the photographed frontage is a game-layout proposal.</td>
</tr>
</tbody></table></div>
<p>The room modules can serve different departments. Do not equate “Accounts” with one architectural style or make a real street address synonymous with a department. Place and operational function remain separate objects in the design.</p>
<h2 id="research-offices/colour-and-material-rules"><a class="section-anchor" href="#research-offices/colour-and-material-rules">Colour and material rules</a></h2>
<p>The renderer uses material-specific ramps and additional face shading. These are <strong>current architectural colour examples judged by eye</strong>, not sampled paint specifications or an exhaustive palette:</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>Painted industrial masonry:</strong> dark <code>#343C3B</code>, shadow face <code>#495451</code>, lit face <code>#606B64</code>, edge <code>#8A9386</code>. Tonal courses articulate material without flattening the wall.</li>
<li><strong>Pale plaster:</strong> shadow <code>#7C887C</code>, middle <code>#A6B0A1</code>, lit face <code>#D1D4C3</code>, edge <code>#EEE9D5</code>. Separate white metal, desk and glazing ramps remain in the component renderer.</li>
<li><strong>Pale timber:</strong> <code>#806746</code>, <code>#B99A69</code>, <code>#D0B786</code>, <code>#E7D5AA</code>. Broad panels and sparse seam/grain marks carry the material; enlarged inspection exposes the same native pixels.</li>
<li><strong>Orange metal / coral upholstery:</strong> metal <code>#8B372B</code>, <code>#D9512D</code>, <code>#F17A47</code>; upholstery may be softer but retains a separate shadow face. Keep this as a visible material, not the universal alert colour.</li>
<li><strong>Glass and painted metal accents:</strong> subdued blue/green panes, near-black frames, limited teal and dusty pink furnishings. Deep green plants provide organic contrast against the white hall.</li>
</ul>
<p>Use colour in large, meaningful objects: rail, booth, inset, door, divider. Do not tint whole buildings by department. Saturation can approach RCT’s clarity without turning every facade into a sweet shop.</p>
<h2 id="research-offices/next-steps-spatial-and-recognition-tests"><a class="section-anchor" href="#research-offices/next-steps-spatial-and-recognition-tests">Next steps: spatial and recognition tests</a></h2>
<ol>
<li><strong>Shell recognition:</strong> use the completed B-01, B-02 and B-03 sprites at native size with labels hidden. Check whether viewers distinguish the brick/glass frontage, dark hall and yellow-brick loading frontage.</li>
<li><strong>Interior recognition:</strong> compare each roof-on/off pair at the same camera and scale. Check that timber insert, workstations and Johnson’s mezzanine remain legible without their captions.</li>
<li><strong>Occupation:</strong> test seated work, circulation and handoff states. The present moving people demonstrate scale and occupation, not task completion or four verified work states.</li>
<li><strong>Scene layout:</strong> compare the current composite park with a denser street edge and shared service circulation. Preserve the attraction spaces and planted clearings. This is a layout test, not a decision to replace the theme park with an office replica.</li>
</ol>
<p>A close architectural crop is valuable only if the normal-size sprite carries the same identity. Johnson’s internal mezzanine and the separate orange overlook are implemented. They remain associated with Johnson; Porter’s coral booth and timber insert retain their own sources. The <a href="assets/sprite-catalogue/sheets/buildings-overview.png">current building sheet</a> makes the three roof-on forms directly comparable; individual catalogue entries include their cutaways.</p>
<h2 id="research-offices/supporting-inspected-images-and-research-record"><a class="section-anchor" href="#research-offices/supporting-inspected-images-and-research-record">Supporting inspected images and research record</a></h2>
<ul>
<li><a href="https://images.squarespace-cdn.com/content/v1/5eb3104d0e74237ec948380b/1732580665719-AHSA5W8HNPW4PQMQNJXM/7_8504070.jpg" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Porter timber volume close view</a>: vertical battens, orange recess, black window, room top below main ceiling. Particularly useful while drawing the insert module.</li>
<li><a href="https://images.squarespace-cdn.com/content/v1/5eb3104d0e74237ec948380b/1732580664767-V1LUQQFL74X7NSRVDE4P/5-Capture0001-163.jpg" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Porter long circulation aisle</a>: circulation is framed by room-volumes, signs, white ducts and red service piping; these are not free-standing houses.</li>
<li><a href="https://images.squarespace-cdn.com/content/v1/5eb3104d0e74237ec948380b/1588805933524-ESIMUYDJ3ISJW1TFDY21/6A0A9010.jpg" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Johnson partitions</a>: tall white planes with square openings establish sightlines without closing off the whole hall.</li>
<li><a href="https://images.squarespace-cdn.com/content/v1/5eb3104d0e74237ec948380b/1588805933333-S98Z2SA4VW4MZMOZJ6H5/6A0A9253.jpg" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Johnson tree, bar and stair</a>: material and scale relationships for a roof-off interior.</li>
<li><a href="https://images.squarespace-cdn.com/content/v1/5eb3104d0e74237ec948380b/1588805953307-TRHM1FI68WBK1T21PGAY/IMG_4227.jpg" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Johnson before adaptation</a>: industrial roof and shell were already present; crates and storage describe the earlier state, not the finished office.</li>
<li><a href="research/office-assets/nyc-north-brooklyn-full-low.pdf">NYC report, local PDF</a> and <a href="research/office-assets/nyc-north-brooklyn-printed-p66.png">full-page visual inspection</a>: printed p.66 is PDF page 86. The full page preserves its framing and avoids inventing a photo-specific credit. No individual photographer is credited on the inspected page.</li>

Architectural observations remain separate from case findings. The archive preserves source copies and attribution leads, not a blanket production-use licence.</p>
</article><nav class="chapter-footer" aria-label="Chapter navigation"><a href="#research-refinement"><small>Previous</small>Sprite refinement</a><a href="#research-discoveries"><small>Next</small>Office discoveries →</a></nav></div></section><section class="reader-page" id="page-research-discoveries" hidden aria-labelledby="title-research-discoveries"><aside class="chapter-notes"><div class="note-id">REFERENCE / R5</div><header class="chapter-heading"><h1 id="title-research-discoveries" tabindex="-1">Office discoveries</h1></header><div class="source-preface"><p>Research checked against design 0.10 / art 0.9, 16 September 2026. Sources: inspected photographs in Matt Fry’s locally archived public portfolio, checked against the public project pages and PSF Projects’ project page. These are visual references, not facts about the case. Each entry separates the observed object, its current game interpretation and any further proposed interaction.</p>
<p>The sculpted reception desk, indoor Chrysler and tree under the skylight supply three different silhouettes and spatial conditions: arrival, an obstruction people gather around, and a shared clearing. The park makes the car a circuit attraction and the room name Supernova an orbital pavilion. The inflatable/shark kit changes the courtyard arrangement. Small objects can reward attention through their shape and placement without requiring a dialogue box each.</p>
<p><strong>Reading rule:</strong> “Observed” describes the photograph. “Reported” comes from a named source. “Proposal” is new game design. Proposed game behaviours do not claim what the real office or its occupants did.</p>

Current design decisions are maintained in the game, interface and build chapters. Source limits remain attached to each claim.</p><h2 id="research-discoveries/1-the-sculpted-white-reception-desk-porter"><a class="section-anchor" href="#research-discoveries/1-the-sculpted-white-reception-desk-porter">1. The sculpted white reception desk — Porter</a></h2>
<p><strong>Observed.</strong> A very long, irregular white counter has rounded bulges, scooped concavities and a taller right end. Its surface is densely flecked with charcoal fragments. Plants cluster in the recesses along its foot. Two screens sit against a tall timber wall behind it; a large, shallow, white pendant hangs above. Workshop pictures show the same sculptural form supported on timber, then being worked by hand. The exact compound/material is not established by appearance.</p>
<p><strong>Evidence.</strong> <a href="assets/office-references/porter-reception.jpg">Installed front desk</a>; <a href="https://images.squarespace-cdn.com/content/v1/5eb3104d0e74237ec948380b/1739922190330-8PTENPE6N4X5VDFXUDUY/IMG_0879.jpg" target="_blank" rel="noopener">workshop state</a>; <a href="https://images.squarespace-cdn.com/content/v1/5eb3104d0e74237ec948380b/1739922191235-A1SOT0D2E4RHWGKZQ2QN/IMG_0891.jpg" target="_blank" rel="noopener">fabrication in progress</a>; <a href="https://images.squarespace-cdn.com/content/v1/5eb3104d0e74237ec948380b/1739922435406-PBVE3GGFD6PWVO0A7DYP/IMG_0964.jpg" target="_blank" rel="noopener">surface close-up</a>.</p>
<p><strong>Reported.</strong> Matt’s page groups this sequence under “Front desk.” His page describes his involvement in design, fabrication management and art; it does not assign the fabrication of this particular object to a named person. <a href="https://mattfryed.com/65porter" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Matt Fry, 65 Porter</a>.</p>
<p><strong>Current study.</strong> L-07 makes the counter an open-air arrival landmark, using unequal heights, curved lobes, shaded hollows and sparse surface flecks. The source&#39;s exact material is not assigned. <strong>Next steps:</strong> test a visitor queue along the concave face and a receptionist visible behind it; construction stages remain proposed.</p>
<figure class="inline-example"><a href="assets/sprite-catalogue/index.html#L-07"><img src="assets/sprite-catalogue/objects/L-07/default.png" alt="Current sculpted reception sprite"></a><figcaption>Current sculpted reception sprite<a href="assets/sprite-catalogue/index.html#L-07">Inspect L-07 ↗</a></figcaption></figure>
<p><strong>Credit.</strong> Portfolio source: Matt Fry. Photographer and object-specific maker not identified in the inspected source.</p>
<h2 id="research-discoveries/2-a-full-sized-white-chrysler-indoors-johnson"><a class="section-anchor" href="#research-discoveries/2-a-full-sized-white-chrysler-indoors-johnson">2. A full-sized white Chrysler indoors — Johnson</a></h2>
<p><strong>Observed.</strong> A long white four-door car sits across the office’s circulation space. Chrome trim, red rear lamps, a dark cabin and four round front lamps make it readable from both ends. The Chrysler lettering is visible. It appears with desks, a mezzanine and orange stair; a second image shows the front with an inflatable castle behind it. This establishes presence, not ownership, operating condition or why it was there.</p>
<p><strong>Evidence.</strong> <a href="assets/office-references/johnson-mezzanine.jpg">Rear three-quarter view</a>; <a href="assets/office-references/johnson-inflatable.jpg">front view</a>. A white car is also partly visible in <a href="assets/office-references/porter-gathering.jpg">Porter’s kitchen view</a>; the photographs alone do not establish that it is the same vehicle.</p>
<p><strong>Current study.</strong> L-01 is a long white sedan with 16 orientations, sloped glazing, recessed wheels and selective chrome. It can remain parked or run the invented L-10 circuit with a planted island and boarding apron. The full assembly makes the theme-park interpretation visible. It carries no financial symbolism. Pedestrian gathering, seating and ride boarding are not simulated.</p>
<figure class="inline-example"><a href="assets/sprite-catalogue/index.html#L-10"><img src="assets/sprite-catalogue/objects/L-10/default.png" alt="Current invented Chrysler attraction assembly"></a><figcaption>Current invented Chrysler attraction assembly<a href="assets/sprite-catalogue/index.html#L-10">Inspect L-10 ↗</a></figcaption></figure>
<p><strong>Credit.</strong> Johnson images: Matt Fry portfolio; photographer not explicitly credited on the project page. The filename “JoeCarrotta” is retained as provenance, not treated as a verified authorship statement. Porter kitchen image: Zach Pontz, matched to PSF’s gallery.</p>
<h2 id="research-discoveries/3-inflatable-castle-and-suspended-shark-johnson"><a class="section-anchor" href="#research-discoveries/3-inflatable-castle-and-suspended-shark-johnson">3. Inflatable castle and suspended shark — Johnson</a></h2>
<p><strong>Observed.</strong> A red/yellow/blue/green inflatable castle occupies the end of the hall, directly behind the Chrysler. Its four pointed corner turrets reach towards the rafters. A large, dark shark-shaped balloon hangs below a skylight. Small coloured balloons are caught high among the roof members; others lie at floor level. Bunting runs along the orange mezzanine railing.</p>
<p><strong>Evidence.</strong> <a href="assets/office-references/johnson-inflatable.jpg">Daytime view of castle, shark and car</a>; <a href="https://images.squarespace-cdn.com/content/v1/5eb3104d0e74237ec948380b/1588805943464-SBZMVEB3NV96UJKMRP5C/JoeCarrotta-0177.jpg" target="_blank" rel="noopener">occupied evening view</a>.</p>
<p><strong>Reported.</strong> The project page includes these photographs without an event caption or date. Do not infer that the inflatable was a permanent office facility, or that this is the event described in entry 5. <a href="https://mattfryed.com/266-johnson" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Matt Fry, 266 Johnson</a>.</p>
<p><strong>Current study.</strong> The courtyard switches between booths, garden and inflatable layouts. The castle has rounded inflated columns, seams and entrance netting; the separately drawn shark has a restrained floating motion. This is an authored layout switch, not a simulation of inflation, crowd arrival or path congestion. <strong>Next steps:</strong> test whether a temporary event should reserve floor space or change physical routes in the operating game.</p>
<p><strong>Credit.</strong> Matt Fry portfolio. Original filenames contain JoeCarrotta; project page supplies no explicit photographer credit.</p>
<h2 id="research-discoveries/4-the-tabby-in-the-sun-patch-johnson"><a class="section-anchor" href="#research-discoveries/4-the-tabby-in-the-sun-patch-johnson">4. The tabby in the sun patch — Johnson</a></h2>
<p><strong>Observed.</strong> A brown tabby lies on the floor with its head bent towards a forepaw. It has a purple collar with purple and green tags. The orange stair and desk chairs remain visible behind it; a hard-edged patch of sunlight cuts across the floor. No name or ownership is established. Do not transcribe small contact text from the collar.</p>
<p><strong>Evidence.</strong> <a href="assets/office-references/johnson-cat.jpg">Cat under the skylight</a>.</p>
<p><strong>Current study.</strong> L-06 has a resting profile, forepaws, lowered head, tabby marks, purple collar and four sampled tail poses. It remains readable with motion paused. Paw-washing, loafing and walking between sun patches are not implemented. <strong>Next steps:</strong> compare its scenic scale with nearby staff and decide whether additional ambient states add enough to justify them. It has no task marker, dialogue or case-solving role.</p>
<figure class="inline-example"><a href="assets/sprite-catalogue/index.html#L-06"><img src="assets/sprite-catalogue/objects/L-06/tail-0.png" alt="Resting tabby from the current catalogue"></a><figcaption>Resting tabby from the current catalogue<a href="assets/sprite-catalogue/index.html#L-06">Inspect L-06 ↗</a></figcaption></figure>
<p><strong>Credit.</strong> Matt Fry portfolio; photographer not identified in the inspected source.</p>
<h2 id="research-discoveries/5-the-arcade-cabinet-beside-the-car-johnson"><a class="section-anchor" href="#research-discoveries/5-the-arcade-cabinet-beside-the-car-johnson">5. The arcade cabinet beside the car — Johnson</a></h2>
<p><strong>Observed.</strong> A substantial upright arcade cabinet has a dotted illuminated header and bright vertical light strips. The car sits immediately behind it. A projected game screen on the far wall shows two sides of a court and a 1–1 score. Several people face the cabinet. Cabinet title lettering is partly obscured.</p>
<p><strong>Evidence.</strong> <a href="https://images.squarespace-cdn.com/content/v1/5eb3104d0e74237ec948380b/1588805354828-5TRANSSCPTBHG8UIGZNS/Screen+Shot+2020-05-06+at+6.13.54+PM.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Cabinet, car and projected game</a>.</p>
<p><strong>Reported.</strong> Matt captions this image “Volleygon Launch Party.” That supports the event’s identity; the screenshot filename is not the event date. <a href="https://mattfryed.com/266-johnson" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Matt Fry, 266 Johnson</a>.</p>
<p><strong>Next steps — proposed, not implemented.</strong> A small optional playable diversion could occupy a cabinet, with a scene-scale light response and a short queue. It should be entered deliberately and exited immediately. An original tiny game could preserve the observed relationship between game, gathering and architecture without reproducing Volleygon or importing its art.</p>
<p><strong>Credit.</strong> Matt Fry portfolio. Photographer and depicted cabinet’s creators are not established by this photograph/caption.</p>
<h2 id="research-discoveries/6-black-stag-with-bright-antlers-porter"><a class="section-anchor" href="#research-discoveries/6-black-stag-with-bright-antlers-porter">6. Black stag with bright antlers — Porter</a></h2>
<p><strong>Observed.</strong> A nearly life-sized black stag stands at the end of an open desk aisle. Its bright silver-looking antlers extend widely against the pale wall; four thin legs and a compact dark body give it a very different profile from an employee. A mint-coloured manual typewriter also sits on the near cabinet in the same image.</p>
<p><strong>Evidence.</strong> <a href="assets/office-references/porter-hall.jpg">Stag and mint typewriter</a>; <a href="https://images.squarespace-cdn.com/content/v1/5eb3104d0e74237ec948380b/1732580662631-R8PD63I3ANVKFOG8ADZC/2-Capture0003-307.jpg" target="_blank" rel="noopener">stag through glazing</a>.</p>
<p><strong>Current study.</strong> L-02 enlarges the stag into a fixed park landmark on an invented plinth. The near-frontal dark body, separated legs and bright branching antlers carry its identity. <strong>Next steps:</strong> assess the silhouette with surrounding people and planting; the mint typewriter remains an undrawn incidental-object candidate.</p>
<p><strong>Reported / credit.</strong> The sculpture’s maker, title and reason for placement are not given. Photos: Zach Pontz, matched by image filename and content to <a href="https://www.psfprojects.com/workplace/madwell-creative-agency" target="_blank" rel="noopener">PSF’s gallery</a>.</p>
<h2 id="research-discoveries/7-pink-circular-neon-with-an-animal-outline-porter"><a class="section-anchor" href="#research-discoveries/7-pink-circular-neon-with-an-animal-outline-porter">7. Pink circular neon with an animal outline — Porter</a></h2>
<p><strong>Observed.</strong> A pink circular neon sign encloses a white outlined animal on a white wall above a low ramp/partition. Its reflection appears in adjacent black-framed glazing. The exact animal and the sign’s maker are not securely identifiable at the available viewing scale.</p>
<p><strong>Evidence.</strong> <a href="https://images.squarespace-cdn.com/content/v1/5eb3104d0e74237ec948380b/1732580668856-HTBIUCN5YMXMSUINLIG2/Capture0022-126.jpg" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Neon above the ramp</a>.</p>
<p><strong>Next steps — proposed, not implemented.</strong> A compact wayfinding light could use a pink ring, a small pale interior mark and a single tinted wall patch. It could orient the player in the roof-off view. The animal, maker and meaning remain unresolved; the sign should not acquire an invented departmental identity.</p>
<p><strong>Credit.</strong> Photograph: Zach Pontz via PSF/Matt Fry project galleries. Sign authorship and meaning unverified.</p>
<h2 id="research-discoveries/8-a-tree-occupying-the-skylight-johnson"><a class="section-anchor" href="#research-discoveries/8-a-tree-occupying-the-skylight-johnson">8. A tree occupying the skylight — Johnson</a></h2>
<p><strong>Observed.</strong> A tall, comparatively airy deciduous-looking tree rises from a large white rectangular planter within the desk floor. Its upper branches meet the skylight’s light opening. The raised planter has a stepped white edge. The tree, planter, skylight and surrounding chairs make one spatial arrangement; it is not a generic outdoor park tree transplanted indoors.</p>
<p><strong>Evidence.</strong> <a href="assets/office-references/johnson-mezzanine.jpg">Tree, white planter and roof opening</a>; <a href="https://images.squarespace-cdn.com/content/v1/5eb3104d0e74237ec948380b/1588805933333-S98Z2SA4VW4MZMOZJ6H5/6A0A9253.jpg" target="_blank" rel="noopener">opposite view beside the bar</a>.</p>
<p><strong>Reported.</strong> Porter separately contains an indoor garden that PSF calls the Solarium, beside the kitchen and eating area. That is a different site and should not be used to name Johnson’s tree. <a href="https://www.psfprojects.com/workplace/madwell-creative-agency" target="_blank" rel="noopener">PSF Projects</a>.</p>
<p><strong>Current study.</strong> Johnson’s cutaway contains a tree in a white planter; Porter’s Solarium becomes a separate invented glass pavilion. These source associations remain distinct. <strong>Next steps:</strong> test an airy indoor canopy, the aligned roof opening and floor light together; a pause point or meeting use remains a proposed operating behavior. The current park tree component is an interpretation, not a reconstruction of Johnson&#39;s species or planting arrangement.</p>
<p><strong>Credit.</strong> Johnson photographs: Matt Fry portfolio; photographer not established. PSF source supplies Porter’s Solarium description, not Johnson’s tree identification.</p>
<h2 id="research-discoveries/9-coral-brackets-and-white-plywood-dividers-porter"><a class="section-anchor" href="#research-discoveries/9-coral-brackets-and-white-plywood-dividers-porter">9. Coral brackets and white plywood dividers — Porter</a></h2>
<p><strong>Observed.</strong> A close-up shows white-faced plywood panels with exposed layered edges, a large circular hole, a coral-pink metal bracket with rounded ends and visible screws, and a slotted white metal floor support. Wide photographs show white cabinet/divider runs, circular black pulls, coloured upright posts and desk plants.</p>
<p><strong>Evidence.</strong> <a href="https://images.squarespace-cdn.com/content/v1/5eb3104d0e74237ec948380b/1739921816128-NF79EUPODKVFWZZDB6OT/E0D74F78-3861-4549-8A54-9140639C361B.JPG" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Joinery close-up</a>; <a href="assets/office-references/porter-hall.jpg">assembled desk floor</a>.</p>
<p><strong>Reported.</strong> PSF credits Millwright with collaborative millwork design/fabrication. Matt credits his own design and fabrication-management involvement. These broad credits do not identify the author of this one bracket.</p>
<p><strong>Current study.</strong> M-01 combines a white slab, warm ply edge, coloured supports, screens and separate chairs. The component can repeat along a row. <strong>Next steps:</strong> test cabinet/divider variants and an upgrade that adds one bay without breaking the occupied group. The photographed bracket remains a construction reference; it is not a decorative border applied throughout the interface.</p>
<p><strong>Credit.</strong> Close-up photographer unspecified in Matt’s source. Wide desk-floor photograph: Zach Pontz. Architecture/millwork credits retained above.</p>
<h2 id="research-discoveries/10-named-rooms-saturated-recesses-and-large-paintings-porter"><a class="section-anchor" href="#research-discoveries/10-named-rooms-saturated-recesses-and-large-paintings-porter">10. Named rooms, saturated recesses and large paintings — Porter</a></h2>
<p><strong>Observed.</strong> The SUPERNOVA sign sits above a black-framed glazed meeting-room door. Amber curtains and a low broad pendant sit inside; a large painting of tightly packed, brightly coloured ghost-like figures hangs beside it and reflects in the glass. Elsewhere a MAGMA sign marks an orange-red recess; GROVE is visible on another room. The room names come from readable signage, not filenames.</p>
<p><strong>Evidence.</strong> <a href="assets/office-references/porter-supernova.jpg">SUPERNOVA, curtain and painting</a>; <a href="https://images.squarespace-cdn.com/content/v1/5eb3104d0e74237ec948380b/1732580664767-V1LUQQFL74X7NSRVDE4P/5-Capture0001-163.jpg" target="_blank" rel="noopener">MAGMA recess</a>; <a href="https://images.squarespace-cdn.com/content/v1/5eb3104d0e74237ec948380b/1732580667965-9KM29A8YNSQ29UIXGV7H/Capture0019-102.jpg" target="_blank" rel="noopener">GROVE doorway</a>. <a href="https://images.squarespace-cdn.com/content/v1/5eb3104d0e74237ec948380b/1732580665943-FDO8SVBWFTZA4QLA0FY7/8-Capture0001-251.jpg" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Coral booth sequence</a> adds small portrait blocks and black/white globe pendants to the same room-scale vocabulary.</p>
<p><strong>Current study.</strong> Timber-room M-02 retains the coloured doorway pocket. Supernova becomes L-03, a black-and-silver roadside pavilion with ribbed lower panels, a chrome canopy, bright fascia lettering and an invented silver orbit around a warm sphere. <strong>Next steps:</strong> decide whether meeting occupancy warrants a curtain state. Paintings, portraits and other named rooms are not implemented as a complete room set; photographed art must not be imported as game texture.</p>
<figure class="inline-example"><a href="assets/sprite-catalogue/index.html#L-03"><img src="assets/sprite-catalogue/objects/L-03/default.png" alt="Supernova meeting pavilion with invented orbital sign"></a><figcaption>Supernova meeting pavilion with invented orbital sign<a href="assets/sprite-catalogue/index.html#L-03">Inspect L-03 ↗</a></figcaption></figure>
<p><strong>Credit.</strong> Photos: Zach Pontz via matched PSF gallery. Painting/portrait artists and pendant manufacturers not established; photographed artworks are references, not reusable game textures.</p>
<h2 id="research-discoveries/current-park-study"><a class="section-anchor" href="#research-discoveries/current-park-study">Current park study</a></h2>
<p>The assembled map now uses Boerum, Porter and Johnson-derived building families. It includes original sprites for the counter, Chrysler, stag, Supernova pavilion, Solarium, orange overlook, tabby and inflatable/shark event kit. The inspector shows a source photograph beside the proposed use.</p>
<p>The parked car and optional miniature circuit are both available; the circuit is explicitly invented. Booths, garden and inflatable are alternative courtyard layouts, not a historical sequence. The Volleygon cabinet, neon and typewriter remain proposed additions.</p>
<h2 id="research-discoveries/next-steps-scene-curation"><a class="section-anchor" href="#research-discoveries/next-steps-scene-curation">Next steps: scene curation</a></h2>
<ul>
<li><strong>Three major anchors:</strong> sculpted counter at arrival, car/circuit, and tree/planter. Check that each remains legible with labels hidden; test the indoor car placement separately from the implemented outdoor attraction.</li>
<li><strong>One changing occupation:</strong> inflatable/shark party kit or arcade gathering, not every observed event at once.</li>
<li><strong>Quiet discoveries:</strong> retain the implemented tabby and stag; compare the proposed neon and mint typewriter before expanding the set.</li>
<li><strong>Repeatable structure:</strong> white industrial frame, timber meeting-room volumes, saturated recesses, coral joinery, white desks. Avoid spreading the strongest object colours evenly across every building.</li>
<li><strong>Chronology stays explicit:</strong> Johnson’s orange exposed mezzanine stair, tree and party photos do not prove these same arrangements at Porter. Porter has its own enclosed orange stair, skylit social area and sculpted reception.</li>
<li><strong>No trailer verified:</strong> none of the 34 Porter and 11 Johnson gallery files inspected here establishes a trailer. A trailer would require another source or be labelled an original addition.</li>
</ul>
<p>Use original sprite interpretation of these observed forms. Photographs and depicted artworks are retained as credited research sources; the archive does not itself establish a production reuse licence.</p>
<h2 id="research-discoveries/source-and-credit-record"><a class="section-anchor" href="#research-discoveries/source-and-credit-record">Source and credit record</a></h2>
<ol>
<li><a href="https://mattfryed.com/65porter" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Matt Fry — 65 Porter</a>: creator’s project page; design/fabrication-management account; architecture credited to PSF Projects Architecture DPC, construction to PBS Solutions, fabrication to Millwright. Local metadata: <a href="https://mattfryed.com/65porter" target="_blank" rel="noopener">65porter.json</a>, including the original public URL for each archived image.</li>
<li><a href="https://mattfryed.com/266-johnson" target="_blank" rel="noopener">Matt Fry — 266 Johnson</a>: creator’s design/construction account and office gallery; explicit Volleygon event caption. Local metadata: <a href="https://mattfryed.com/65porter" target="_blank" rel="noopener">266-johnson.json</a>.</li>
<li><a href="https://www.psfprojects.com/workplace/madwell-creative-agency" target="_blank" rel="noopener">PSF Projects — Madwell Creative Agency</a>: architect’s description, project credits and gallery; names Zach Pontz as photographer. This credit is applied only to matching images, not extended to Matt’s entire archive.</li>
</ol>
</article><nav class="chapter-footer" aria-label="Chapter navigation"><a href="#research-offices"><small>Previous</small>Office architecture</a><a href="#research-park"><small>Next</small>Park interaction study →</a></nav></div></section><section class="reader-page" id="page-research-park" hidden aria-labelledby="title-research-park"><aside class="chapter-notes"><div class="note-id">REFERENCE / R6</div><header class="chapter-heading"><h1 id="title-research-park" tabindex="-1">Park interaction study</h1></header><div class="source-preface"><p>Checked against design 0.10 / art 0.9, 16 September 2026. This separates implemented scenic interactions from proposed operating behavior. The original 0.5 review is archived. These observations concern architecture, objects and interface behavior, not the verification of financial claims.</p>
<p><strong>Make the campus an assembled industrial pleasure ground.</strong> Three distinctive sites, a few improbable furnishings, visible human occupation and one clear circulation system will carry more character than additional lawn, identical trees or miniature signs. The attraction is getting close to the objects and seeing how the place works. Every landmark does not need to mint revenue.</p>
</div><nav class="page-links" aria-label="In Park interaction study"><a href="#research-park/three-interaction-studies"><span>01</span>Three interaction studies</a><a href="#research-park/current-arrangement-and-a-layout-alternative-to-test"><span>02</span>Current arrangement and a layout alternative to test</a><a href="#research-park/selection-and-meaning"><span>03</span>Selection and meaning</a><a href="#research-park/current-behavior-and-remaining-gaps"><span>04</span>Current behavior and remaining gaps</a><a href="#research-park/next-steps-acceptance-checks"><span>05</span>Next steps: acceptance checks</a></nav><div class="release-note">DESIGN 0.10 / ART 0.9<br><a href="previews/index.html">Current previews ↗</a><br><a href="assets/sprite-catalogue/index.html">Object catalogue ↗</a><br><a href="archive/index.html">Archive ↗</a></div><div class="document-tools"><a href="research/park-logic-review.md">Editable source ↗</a></div></aside><div class="chapter-main"><article class="article-body"><p class="reference-status">Supporting research and binding notes. Current design decisions are maintained in the game, interface and build chapters. Source limits remain attached to each claim.</p><h2 id="research-park/three-interaction-studies"><a class="section-anchor" href="#research-park/three-interaction-studies">Three interaction studies</a></h2>
<h3 id="research-park/1-white-chrysler-display-loop-and-object-inspection"><a class="section-anchor" href="#research-park/1-white-chrysler-display-loop-and-object-inspection">1. White Chrysler: display loop and object inspection</a></h3>
<p>The long white car occupies an invented paved circuit with a planted island and boarding apron. The visible loop makes it a small attraction. The delivery van remains a separate asset with a different purpose; neither vehicle carries case money or documents through the scene.</p>
<p><strong>Implemented:</strong> select the Chrysler through the scene or landmark list, inspect the office photograph, and switch between the parked state and running circuit. Its catalogue entry exposes 16 orientations. The global motion control freezes the current animation time; <strong>Park the Chrysler</strong> instead returns it to a designated parked point. Opening the practice record pauses world motion. No passenger boarding, attraction revenue, personal collection cabinet or evidence-point reward is implemented.</p>
<figure class="inline-example"><a href="assets/sprite-catalogue/index.html#L-10"><img src="assets/sprite-catalogue/objects/L-10/default.png" alt="The complete invented Chrysler circuit"></a><figcaption>The complete invented Chrysler circuit<a href="assets/sprite-catalogue/index.html#L-10">Inspect L-10 ↗</a></figcaption></figure>
<p><strong>Why it earns its space:</strong> a memorable moving landmark supplies orientation and a miniature spectacle. It gives the player a reason to explore close-up art without requiring a modal interruption. The visible closed loop makes its decorative purpose intelligible.</p>
<p><strong>Limit:</strong> the photographs establish a white car as furnishing. Its motion and outdoor display are design fiction. Do not animate it travelling between named offices: seeing a similar car in different source photographs does not establish a relocation history. No passenger icons for clients, suppliers or transfers. The car carries the car.</p>
<h3 id="research-park/2-coral-gathering-booth-proposed-service-frontage"><a class="section-anchor" href="#research-park/2-coral-gathering-booth-proposed-service-frontage">2. Coral gathering booth; proposed service frontage</a></h3>
<p><strong>Implemented:</strong> the current booth is Porter’s coral gathering furniture: pale divider, orange upholstery, table and visible supports. It appears inside Porter and in the courtyard’s <strong>Booths</strong> layout. This is not the trailer/service booth proposed in the earlier review. No trailer was verified in the inspected office galleries.</p>
<figure class="inline-example"><a href="assets/sprite-catalogue/index.html#M-03"><img src="assets/sprite-catalogue/objects/M-03/default.png" alt="The current coral gathering booth"></a><figcaption>The current coral gathering booth<a href="assets/sprite-catalogue/index.html#M-03">Inspect M-03 ↗</a></figcaption></figure>
<p><strong>Next steps — optional route study:</strong> design a separate service counter with <strong>Court side</strong> and <strong>Service side</strong> access. Show the two frontage states and a physical handoff route preview. Reserve enough clear ground for both choices; one might be shorter for the workshop, the other easier to reach from the entrance. A parcel-carrying worker could demonstrate the route. None of these controls or route consequences is currently implemented.</p>
<p><strong>Later operating use:</strong> if physical handoffs enter the prototype, let this choice alter only a stated carrying distance or reserved handoff slot. The player chooses which work to place near which access point. The route need not be a new path-laying system.</p>
<p><strong>Why test it:</strong> a reversible layout decision could make the RCT pleasure of arranging entrances and service edges consequential at a small scale.</p>
<p><strong>Limit:</strong> a parcel is a physical deliverable. Documents, approval rights and money do not travel at walking speed. Do not label an ambient queue “unpaid suppliers” or turn a booth visit into payment confirmation.</p>
<h3 id="research-park/3-orange-stair-and-tree-an-overlook-that-reveals-the-assembly"><a class="section-anchor" href="#research-park/3-orange-stair-and-tree-an-overlook-that-reveals-the-assembly">3. Orange stair and tree: an overlook that reveals the assembly</a></h3>
<p>The current orange overlook is a separate park landmark derived from Johnson’s stair and mezzanine. Johnson’s cutaway contains a connected orange stair and mezzanine, a central white birch and the office cat. Porter’s Solarium is a different source association and a separate invented glass pavilion. These objects do not establish one historical room or shared floor plan.</p>
<p><strong>Implemented:</strong> every building has paired <strong>Exterior / Interior</strong> preview buttons above the scene. Johnson’s interior is exposed initially. The overlook also provides <strong>Open Johnson interior</strong>. Both paths preserve the building footprint and use the same renderer as the catalogue. The assembly demonstration exposes construction stages. <strong>Next steps:</strong> test independent workstation inspection and a durable return bookmark; those controls are not implemented.</p>
<p><strong>Why it earns its space:</strong> the player learns the place by taking it apart and putting it back together. The exaggerated overlook can feel like an observation attraction without pretending to be an actual ride. It also demonstrates the modular art system rather than hiding it under a finished roof.</p>
<p><strong>Limit:</strong> the overlook changes visibility, not authority or historical knowledge. A higher camera grants no access to private records. Tree, rail and meeting room are separate objects; none stands in for a company. Keep essential interactions reachable in the object list even with the roof on.</p>
<h2 id="research-park/current-arrangement-and-a-layout-alternative-to-test"><a class="section-anchor" href="#research-park/current-arrangement-and-a-layout-alternative-to-test">Current arrangement and a layout alternative to test</a></h2>
<p>The current campus has three named site modules, selectable landmarks, paved aprons, a circulation grid and planted areas. Johnson sits forward with an open sightline; its sign is on the forecourt, and tall foreground trees have been removed. Supernova sits apart from the stag so both silhouettes remain visible. Boerum, Porter and Johnson use different architectural forms; the old creative/production/accounts aliases survive only as internal specimen keys. They are not the displayed site taxonomy.</p>
<p>The remaining alternative is a denser street-and-court arrangement:</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>Foreground proposal:</strong> joined Boerum frontage, continuous pavement and the anonymous pacing pedestrian.</li>
<li><strong>Middle proposal:</strong> shared court, Chrysler loop and a separate service apron. Test clear sightlines to entrances before adding an optional service counter.</li>
<li><strong>Rear proposal:</strong> Porter hall and Johnson stair/tree volume, with taller objects positioned to preserve foreground visibility. Keep material and architecture separate from department assignments.</li>
</ul>
<p>This is a <strong>composite design study</strong>, not a map of historically adjacent or simultaneously occupied offices. State that once beside the scene and retain each source association in its site inspector. Do not invent roads, real travel times or tenancy chronology between the modules. Compressing and recombining architecture is acceptable; changing its evidentiary meaning is not.</p>
<p>Compare that proposal with the current arrangement using label-free recognition, object selection and visible entrances. Preserve the miniature attractions and greenery that make it a park. A future service counter needs an intelligible handoff edge; the existing gathering booth needs seating access. They solve different spatial problems.</p>
<h2 id="research-park/selection-and-meaning"><a class="section-anchor" href="#research-park/selection-and-meaning">Selection and meaning</a></h2>
<p>Keep three levels explicit:</p>
<div class="table-scroll" tabindex="0" role="region" aria-label="Scrollable specification table"><table>
<thead>
<tr>
<th>Selected thing</th>
<th>Inspector answers</th>
<th>Must not imply</th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody><tr>
<td>Site module</td>
<td>Which architectural source, which spaces, which modeled occupancy?</td>
<td>Address = department or legal entity</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Scenic object</td>
<td>What is it, what can be changed, which source inspired it?</td>
<td>Furnishing = transaction or evidence</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Project/department assignment</td>
<td>Which work, people, capacity and commitments?</td>
<td>A permanent project building or one department per site</td>
</tr>
</tbody></table></div>
<p>Selecting Porter should not rename the inspector “Accounts.” Departments can occupy several modules and share one. Entity boundaries belong to the relevant view and dated records, not a fence added around a roof. A collectible is an inspected visual object, never a recovered case exhibit by default.</p>
<h2 id="research-park/current-behavior-and-remaining-gaps"><a class="section-anchor" href="#research-park/current-behavior-and-remaining-gaps">Current behavior and remaining gaps</a></h2>
<ol>
<li><strong>Building and landmark selection exist.</strong> Six visible exterior/interior choices cover three sites. The landmark list covers all eight selectable scenic objects; selecting the inflatable restores its courtyard layout. Buildings use polygon hit regions and landmarks use proximity regions. Individual workstations, the shark and the interior birch are not separately selectable.</li>
<li><strong>The inspector keeps one structure.</strong> Every selection shows its sprite, source photograph, observed source features and park interpretation. The financial fixture has a separate, persistent <strong>Practice documents</strong> entry. It is not assigned to a real office.</li>
<li><strong>Sites and departments are separated.</strong> Building controls and signs identify Boerum, Porter and 266 Johnson. No allocation editor is implemented. Shirt colour does not establish employment status.</li>
<li><strong>Animation demonstrates occupation and spectacle.</strong> Walking, the circuit and shark motion do not advance work, cash, queues or records. The separate Interface chapter demonstrates the proposed five-view model.</li>
<li><strong>Reading has a return path.</strong> Opening the practice documents pauses motion and replaces the explorer with the document desk. <strong>Return to park</strong> restores selection, exterior/interior state, courtyard, focus and scroll position. Motion stays paused. Production pins, bookmarks and saved games remain future work.</li>
</ol>
<h2 id="research-park/next-steps-acceptance-checks"><a class="section-anchor" href="#research-park/next-steps-acceptance-checks">Next steps: acceptance checks</a></h2>
<ul>
<li>With labels hidden, can a viewer distinguish the street frontage, long hall and stair/tree volume?</li>
<li>Can they select a landmark and its associated site without repeatedly selecting the wrong object? Test workstation selection only after it exists.</li>
<li>After switching the courtyard layout, can they state what changed? Test the separate service-frontage proposal only after its two routes are implemented.</li>
<li>After using the overlook, do they understand it changed the view rather than their permissions?</li>
<li>Can every inspection and route preview be reached with motion disabled and without pixel hunting?</li>
</ul>
<p>The implemented interactions make the park worth inspecting and show how its component system works. The optional service-frontage study proposes a later operational consequence; the present gathering booth and courtyard controls do not simulate one.</p>
</article><nav class="chapter-footer" aria-label="Chapter navigation"><a href="#research-discoveries"><small>Previous</small>Office discoveries</a><a href="#binding"><small>Next</small>Episode 01 binding →</a></nav></div></section><section class="reader-page" id="page-binding" hidden aria-labelledby="title-binding"><aside class="chapter-notes"><div class="note-id">REFERENCE / B01</div><header class="chapter-heading"><h1 id="title-binding" tabindex="-1">Episode 01 binding</h1></header><div class="source-preface"><p>Source-binding draft · 16 September 2026</p>
<p><strong>Bound at export-field level:</strong> a $23,500 Creative Peaks LLC invoice/payment representation; October 21 funding debit; October 22 return credit; Paid/PaidInFull fields in later BILL exports; and a separate October 23 QBO entry crediting Chase and debiting clearing account 1072. Each item has an exact file/row locator below. <strong>Not yet bound:</strong> the original bank pages, an identifier-complete return-to-payment join, the October 23 bank direction, final supplier receipt or later cure, and the ordinary pooling control.</p>
<p><strong>Recommendation:</strong> retain Creative Peaks as the first historical payment-state comparison, conditional on these joins. It is suitable for asking what a payment attempt did and what the platform continued to report. It is not yet a historical production-management episode. The fictional fabrication job must remain fictional.</p>

Current design decisions are maintained in the game, interface and build chapters. Source limits remain attached to each claim.</p><h2 id="binding/1-what-was-inspected"><a class="section-anchor" href="#binding/1-what-was-inspected">1. What was inspected</a></h2>
<p>The reconstruction is <strong>CASE-01</strong>, <code>The_Zero_Machine_Specific_Story_Reconstruction FINAL.pdf</code>, a 58-page counsel briefing dated September 2, 2026. Its SHA-256 was recomputed as <code>9352893711180900fcd770651011c65036504a66570490138d68fcd9a5c253e8</code>. Cached pages 31, 34, 45–48, 52 and 58 match fresh PDF text extraction after Unicode/whitespace normalization. Pages 34 and 45 were also read visually. <code>Design/evidence-map.md</code> supplies the existing design/source context.</p>
<p>The exact CSV records listed below were inspected for this packet. Those are inspected <strong>exports and statement extractions</strong>, not authenticated original statements, invoices, audit logs or recipient confirmations. The source&#39;s labels “proved,” “closed” and “false state” remain attributed conclusions.</p>
<p>File paths below are relative to the external <code>validation_package/</code> source collection. CSV record numbering counts the header as record 1; physical line differences are explicit. Full file hashes, provenance and discovery status are collected in the companion <a href="#binding-inventory">native inventory</a>. An uninspected original is not an original known to be absent.</p>

<h2 id="binding/4-which-connections-can-be-drawn"><a class="section-anchor" href="#binding/4-which-connections-can-be-drawn">4. Which connections can be drawn</a></h2>
<div class="table-scroll" tabindex="0" role="region" aria-label="Scrollable specification table"><table>
<thead>
<tr>
<th>Connection</th>
<th>Present support</th>
<th>Missing before stronger assertion</th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody><tr>
<td>Invoice 8 → payment representation</td>
<td>Vendor, invoice, amount and payment confirmation within BILL; corresponding QBO fields</td>
<td>Stable object IDs, original invoice and historical vendor/profile identity</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Representation → October 21 bank funding</td>
<td>Bank description names Creative Peaks LLC and invoice 8; amounts and dates correspond</td>
<td>Native processor-to-bank identifier bridge; any batch membership</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>October 21 funding → October 22 return</td>
<td>Bank return and BILL funds description agree on amount, direction and referenced funding date</td>
<td>Native reversal/return association; reason code and original statement context</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Return → later Paid/PaidInFull fields</td>
<td>Exported state and source account are compatible with the reported conflict</td>
<td>Complete lifecycle/audit to establish persistence period and distinguish uncured return from later settlement</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Payment → October 23 entry</td>
<td>Same amount; masked QBO confirmation suffixes; inventory reports full matching Chase reference</td>
<td>Original Chase direction; processor receipt and allocation; actual reissue/settlement result</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>Any of the above → supplier never paid, permanent loss, named operator&#39;s purpose</td>
<td><strong>Unsupported here</strong></td>
<td>Recipient evidence, later rail search, dated actor/audit records and separate evidence of intent</td>
</tr>
</tbody></table></div>
<p>Draw bank events as bank events. A bank debit to a processor is not automatically a delivery to the supplier. The visible return credit is not permission to animate the same dollars into a later expense.</p>
<h2 id="binding/5-the-ordinary-control-is-still-an-open-binding-task"><a class="section-anchor" href="#binding/5-the-ordinary-control-is-still-an-open-binding-task">5. The ordinary control is still an open binding task</a></h2>
<p>The inventory now supplies a compact <strong>export-supported comparison</strong>: FT records 348–349 allocate $1,920 and $2,600 to two vendors, totaling $4,520. Both share ACH confirmation <code>015ECWFDPPZCFOP</code> with a single B1 bank debit, record 89, dated February 14, 2019. This is stronger than an amount-only match. It still needs the original statement cents, complete funding-group coverage and the relevant lifecycle records. It is not identified as one of CASE-01’s ten controls. <a href="#binding-inventory/ordinary-pooling-comparison">Inspect the exact rows and limits</a>.</p>
<p>CASE-01 p.45 Appendix G supplies two retrieval leads:</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>Ten ordinary one-bank-event-to-many-BILL allocations, $1,014,217.28 total.</strong> Individual event IDs, dates, member amounts and exhibits are not printed. Select one complete, compact example from its row-level control schedule. Prefer a comparable route when the records support one.</li>
<li><strong>SiriusXM, $150,000 genuine bank payment, four later BILL allocation objects.</strong> This is the named fallback for teaching supported payment with later allocation. The PDF does not establish whether it belongs to the ten-control total. Do not call it a matched pooled control until the bank/member relationship is inspected.</li>
</ul>
<p>The 65 bank-supported versus 43 bank-disproved intercompany routes on p.45 are a different population. The two separate $25,000 events on that page are a useful identity-matching control, not a pooling example. The adjacent Reddit scene on p.34 has a different lifecycle; none of its dates or three-member amounts can fill gaps here.</p>
<p><strong>Current inventory lead, not independently inspected in this packet:</strong> three LOCAL SIXTY NINE invoices, 0000163/0000162/0000167, share a SentPay/disbursement/ACH token chain and total <strong>$50,416.48</strong>. The matching February 3, 2022 bank extraction retains only <strong>$50,416</strong>; its original locator is <code>Madwell_BofA Statements_2022.pdf</code> p.14. This is a promising one-vendor/multiple-invoice allocation, not a verified batch across vendors. Exact cents and original-source closure remain open; see the native inventory.</p>
<p>A valid control must have the original debit, complete member list, allocation arithmetic and enough return/reissue history to support its stated lesson. If its proof stops at processor funding, label that endpoint. Do not promote it to confirmed recipient receipt.</p>
<h2 id="binding/6-what-remains-to-collect"><a class="section-anchor" href="#binding/6-what-remains-to-collect">6. What remains to collect</a></h2>
<ol>
<li><strong>Original BofA statement pp.80/82 and the native return record</strong>, including account, date definitions, reference bridge and return reason. Resolve the reconstruction&#39;s October 21/22 discrepancy explicitly.</li>
<li><strong>BILL payment/bill IDs, funding association and audit history</strong>, including the October 23 update, any retry/reissue and historical payout profile. Export status alone cannot date all transitions.</li>
<li><strong>Original Chase statement and processor funding record</strong> for October 23. Resolve the extraction-direction conflict before presenting a cash arrow.</li>
<li><strong>One complete ordinary control</strong>, from the ten-control schedule or the SiriusXM chain.</li>
<li><strong>Original invoice, contract, acceptance and recipient remittance</strong>, only to the extent needed for claims about service, obligation, delivery or receipt. The exported customer/description fields improve the lead; they do not establish operational consequences.</li>
</ol>
<p>Date of event, date represented in accounting, date of record update and date of observation remain separate. Access, receipt, reading and understanding also remain separate if a real person&#39;s knowledge enters the episode.</p>
<h2 id="binding/7-conservation-play-and-the-fictional-boundary"><a class="section-anchor" href="#binding/7-conservation-play-and-the-fictional-boundary">7. Conservation, play and the fictional boundary</a></h2>
<p>The reported debit and return net to <strong>zero for that pair</strong>: −23,500 + 23,500 = 0. That is not an available-cash calculation or a conclusion about fees, other payments or final loss. Paid/PaidInFull adds no second cash event. The October 23 clearing entry is neither automatic cure nor a second supplier payment. Never add a selected control to the $1,014,217.28 aggregate before checking membership.</p>
<p>In the historical comparison, the player can inspect the records, separate their dates, test identity matches, assemble a bounded account and identify the next necessary exhibit. They cannot change history. A useful answer may end at an unresolved endpoint. “Not supplied” must never score as “never happened.”</p>
<p>In fictional practice, they can reserve capacity, choose in-house or external work, defer a handoff and request a simulated confirmation. <strong>C-07/O-19/P-04, the $100,000 campaign, $12,000 attempted payment, fabricated component, week-three Thursday deadline, team-day capacity, replacement price, approval roles, cash reserves, supplier dialogue and consequences remain invented.</strong> Replacing the supplier&#39;s name with Creative Peaks would not turn them into historical facts. The exported “second 50%” description also does not license treating the synthetic payment as a deposit.</p>
<p>Use separate <code>practice/*</code> and <code>case/*</code> records. Their comparison concerns a mechanism; it is not an identity mapping. A shared screen must not carry a practice field silently into the historical view.</p>
<h2 id="binding/8-readiness-decision"><a class="section-anchor" href="#binding/8-readiness-decision">8. Readiness decision</a></h2>
<p>Creative Peaks remains a compact candidate because its central distinction can be taught without accepting the reconstruction&#39;s enterprise-wide thesis. The date conflict is useful editorial pressure: it forces the interface to distinguish a source&#39;s narration from the records it cites.</p>
<p>Release a historical comparison only at its supported scope: inspect the original return join, establish a later observation of the same BILL object, resolve or visibly retain the October 23 bank-direction issue, and bind one ordinary control. Final supplier receipt may remain open if the episode says so. Complete historical production play requires the separate engagement and delivery chain.</p>
<p>If the ordinary control becomes ready first, test it first. The test is whether a player can explain <strong>one legitimate one-to-many allocation and one returned funding attempt with a later Paid label</strong>, without concluding that every pool is suspect or every return proves permanent nonpayment. No historical readiness is claimed merely because the synthetic prototype works.</p>
<p>Binding files: <a href="#binding-inventory">exact source inventory</a> · <a href="binding/episode-01.bindings.json">structured observations and unresolved links</a>.</p>

<p>All paths below are relative to the external <code>validation_package/</code> source collection. File labels are inventory identifiers, not original exhibit numbers. <code>local</code> provenance applies throughout. The source files remain outside this publication. This inventory preserves file identity and exact locators; a listed path is not a download link.</p>
<p><code>REPRODUCIBILITY/reading/SOURCE_MANIFEST.md</code> declares SHA-256 values for 111 source files. Its historic paths use <code>Bank Statements</code>, <code>Bill.com</code>, and <code>Quickbooks</code>; the local package uses <code>Banks</code>, <code>BILL</code>, and <code>QB</code>. The hashes below were computed from the actual bytes. “Matches” means the computed digest appears in the manifest. This establishes consistency with that manifest, not completeness, correctness, native provenance, the identity of an exporter, or authority over the later CASE-01 account. File modification dates were not used to choose a canonical version. No manifest establishing the authoritative September 2026 corpus was located.</p>

<h2 id="binding-inventory/exact-observation-references"><a class="section-anchor" href="#binding-inventory/exact-observation-references">Exact observation references</a></h2>
<p>CSV record numbers are one-based, counting every parsed record from the beginning of the file. In the ordinary exports, the header is record 1. Physical line numbers were checked separately. They equal record numbers for the cited BILL and bank records. Q1 contains report headers and multiline cells, so both numbers are provided. Its numeric group labels are called <strong>report group labels</strong> here; their status as native QBO transaction IDs has not been established.</p>

<p>Bill description is literally <code>2nd 50% of Muse Video (60&quot; version)</code>. The line is assigned to account <code>COGS - Vendors &amp; Services</code> / <code>50100</code>, department <code>1 New York Office - (Ops/Cogs)</code>, customer <code>ELF23-112S-2 Muse Starfish</code>. These are exported accounting labels; they do not by themselves establish the legal entity that received the work or who controlled payment.</p>
<p>The bill creator field is <code>00601NEQXUFVRIJ6gwek</code>; payment creator is <code>00601ZYFHXSFUIA6dm8c</code>. They are account identifiers, not verified human identities. <code>Vendor Id</code> and bill <code>Integration ID</code> are blank. The payment confirmation is useful across PN/PM and B2; neither export supplies a CP SentPay ID. Do not promote an account label, blank ID, or descriptive match into a stable native entity/object identity.</p>
<h2 id="binding-inventory/conflicts-and-limits-of-the-joins"><a class="section-anchor" href="#binding-inventory/conflicts-and-limits-of-the-joins">Conflicts and limits of the joins</a></h2>
<p><strong>Return date.</strong> CASE-01 p.31 dates the CP return October 21; the binding packet preserves that printed claim. B1 and FN/FM instead distinguish October 21 funding/debit from an October 22 return posting. The return description refers back to October 21. The game must preserve the dates by source and event type until the original statement and payment lifecycle resolve them. A QBO payment date of October 20 is a third kind of date, not an interchangeable timestamp.</p>
<p><strong>Chase direction.</strong> B2&#39;s exact payment confirmation makes it a much stronger link than amount alone. But its <code>IN/DEPOSIT</code> classification conflicts with Q1&#39;s credit to Chase/debit to 1072. A parsing or classification error is possible; it has not been demonstrated. The original Chase statement is required before calling this a verified bank debit or a verified replenishment. Even a confirmed transfer to BILL would not independently prove recipient settlement.</p>
<p><strong>Return identity.</strong> B1&#39;s adjacent equal debit/credit and BILL&#39;s matching funding/return descriptions support a candidate lifecycle. The bank return lacks the CP name and ACH token. Native return trace, funding-transfer ID, or explicit parent-child link is still needed for a unique external join. Separate PN records 12230–12231 concern HITENY S.A. invoice 38, also $23,500, with different confirmations; amount-only matching can attach the wrong transaction.</p>
<p><strong>Status is a snapshot.</strong> May and November exports each say <code>Paid</code> / <code>PaidInFull</code>. That supports the later displayed states. It does not prove the status was unchanged at every intervening moment, who set it, whether another route settled the invoice, recipient receipt, intent, or loss. <code>Cancel Request Submitted=false</code> is also a snapshot field, not proof that no return or other lifecycle event happened.</p>
<p><strong>Missing detail is a coverage result.</strong> FT contains no case-insensitive <code>Creative Peaks</code> match and no match for the CP debit token or payment-confirmation suffix in the targeted search. This establishes a gap in the inspected report, not a missing payment in the world. AU cannot fill it. Do not treat the report&#39;s omission, or a derived <code>FTD_Match=NONE</code>, as evidence of wrongdoing.</p>
<h2 id="binding-inventory/ordinary-pooling-comparison"><a class="section-anchor" href="#binding-inventory/ordinary-pooling-comparison">Ordinary pooling comparison</a></h2>
<p>One compact comparison has an exact shared-token join at export level. It demonstrates why several allocations need not imply several bank withdrawals.</p>
<div class="table-scroll" tabindex="0" role="region" aria-label="Scrollable specification table"><table>
<thead>
<tr>
<th>Reference</th>
<th align="right">FT record/line</th>
<th>Vendor/invoice</th>
<th align="right">Amount</th>
<th>Disbursement / SentPay</th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody><tr>
<td>POOL-MEMBER-A</td>
<td align="right">348</td>
<td><code>Katherine  Beherec</code> / <code>3-14</code></td>
<td align="right">$1,920.00</td>
<td><code>015OZHZFSPZEF5L</code> / <code>stp01JSJYMPZAL1l090n</code></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td>POOL-MEMBER-B</td>
<td align="right">349</td>
<td><code>Scott Nolan</code> / <code>12519</code></td>
<td align="right">$2,600.00</td>
<td><code>015ZXKVSAPZEF5M</code> / <code>stp01TZPQLHJSS1l0912</code></td>
</tr>
</tbody></table></div>
<p>Both FT records have process date <code>02/14/19</code>, funding source Bank of America ending 2105, and ACH confirmation <code>015ECWFDPPZCFOP</code>. Their displayed sum is <strong>$4,520.00</strong>. <code>POOL-BANK</code>: B1 record/line <strong>89</strong>, date <code>2/14/19</code>, shows a $4,520 debit, the same confirmation, and description <code>Multiple Payments</code>. Its original locator is <code>2 Bank of America_Madwell LLC_February 2019.pdf</code>, <code>pageNumber=3</code>.</p>
<p>This is an <strong>export-supported pooled-funding comparison</strong>, not a fully verified legitimate-payment control. The bank extraction displays whole dollars; a displayed equality cannot certify original cents. Native bank/recipient records and coverage of the funding group remain unchecked. It is not asserted to be one of CASE-01&#39;s ten pools or its SiriusXM control. Those populations have not been tied to native records in this pass.</p>
<p>A weaker multi-invoice candidate is FT records/lines 5408–5410: LOCAL SIXTY NINE LLC invoices <code>0000163</code>, <code>0000162</code>, <code>0000167</code>, totaling $50,416.48, shared ACH <code>016DSZHYT23TIT2</code>, disbursement <code>016HCBZLG240BKV</code>, SentPay <code>stp01ZZFCDTFZR3ah5cc</code>, process <code>02/03/22</code>. B1 record/line 3111 shares the token and date but displays only $50,416; locator <code>Madwell_BofA Statements_2022.pdf</code>, page 14. This cannot supply cent-level closure either.</p>
<h2 id="binding-inventory/missing-originals-and-next-binding-requirements"><a class="section-anchor" href="#binding-inventory/missing-originals-and-next-binding-requirements">Missing originals and next binding requirements</a></h2>
<p>The source tree and relevant immediate parent were inventoried without executing their code. Exact-filename searches were also run within the local document and shared-project collections for <code>Madwell_BofA Statements_2024.pdf</code>, <code>20241031-statements-6976-.pdf</code>, and <code>2 Bank of America_Madwell LLC_February 2019.pdf</code>; no matches were returned. This is a bounded filename search, not proof the originals do not exist under other names, in archives, or elsewhere. IX is an inventory aid, not an original-source substitute. Its relevant missing objects were not recovered.</p>
<p>To close the episode: obtain the cited original BoA pages and Chase statement; preserve hashes and actual page locators; obtain the native bill/payment object IDs, complete audit/export with lifecycle timestamps and time zone, and explicit funding/return linkage; identify actor accounts through independent access/user records; and obtain recipient settlement or replacement-payment evidence. The full confirmation links the Chase description to the BILL payment, but the unresolved direction and missing settlement layer remain material.</p>
<p>For the reader now, show literal export fields beside CASE-01 claims, mark the October 21/22 and Chase-direction conflicts, and label the conclusion <strong>recipient settlement unresolved</strong>. A synthetic exercise may demonstrate a return/status mismatch, but it must not borrow a definitive historical ending from the scenario designer.</p>

function navigate(route,{cross=false,focus=true}={}){
 route=normalize(route);if(route===history.state?.route){render(route,undefined,focus);return}
 const fromRoute=history.state?.route||activePage;

 history.pushState({route,scroll:0,index,...inherited,...(cross?{fromRoute,fromTitle,fromIndex:index-1}:{})},'','#'+route);
 render(route,undefined,focus);
}

function search(){

}

function refGroundShadow(points,z=0,opacity=.22,receivers=refGroundShadow.receivers){
 const ink='rgba(24,39,34,'+opacity+')';
 const clip=(input,axis,bound,greater)=>{
  const out=[];for(let i=0;i<input.length;i++){
   const a=input[i],b=input[(i+1)%input.length],ia=greater?a[axis]>=bound:a[axis]<=bound,ib=greater?b[axis]>=bound:b[axis]<=bound;
   if(ia)out.push(a);if(ia!==ib){const t=(bound-a[axis])/(b[axis]-a[axis]);out.push([a[0]+(b[0]-a[0])*t,a[1]+(b[1]-a[1])*t]);}
  }return out;
 };
 const surfaces=receivers;
 if(Array.isArray(surfaces)&&surfaces.length){
  for(const s of surfaces){
   // Intersect each exposed vertical face with the footprint, then shade that face at its own Zs.
   for(const face of s.risers||[]){
    const axis=face.side[0]==='x'?0:1,at=(axis===0?s.x:s.y)+(face.side[1]==='+'?(axis===0?s.w:s.d):0),across=1-axis,hits=[];
    for(let i=0;i<points.length;i++){
     const a=points[i],b=points[(i+1)%points.length];if(Math.abs(a[axis]-at)<1e-8)hits.push(a[across]);
     if((a[axis]<at&&b[axis]>at)||(a[axis]>at&&b[axis]<at)){const t=(at-a[axis])/(b[axis]-a[axis]);hits.push(a[across]+t*(b[across]-a[across]));}
    }
    if(hits.length>=2){
     const start=axis===0?s.y:s.x,length=axis===0?s.d:s.w,lo=Math.max(start,Math.min(...hits)),hi=Math.min(start+length,Math.max(...hits)),bottom=face.bottom??z;
     if(hi>lo&&s.z>bottom){const q=(u,h)=>axis===0?pt(at,u,h):pt(u,at,h);poly([q(lo,bottom),q(hi,bottom),q(hi,s.z),q(lo,s.z)],ink);}
    }
   }
   let p=points;for(const [axis,edge,greater] of [[0,s.x,true],[0,s.x+s.w,false],[1,s.y,true],[1,s.y+s.d,false]])p=clip(p,axis,edge,greater);
   if(p.length>=3)poly(p.map(([u,v])=>pt(u,v,s.z)),ink);
  }
 }else poly(points.map(([u,v])=>pt(u,v,z)),ink);
}
function refLeafVolume(cx,cy,r=10,light=0,joined=false){
 const c=['#263f2d','#385c36','#51773f','#74964e','#9cb65f','#c4ce82'];
 const p=pts=>pts.map(([x,y])=>[cx+Math.round(x*r/10),cy+Math.round(y*r/10)]);
 // Irregular connected leaf masses: shaded lower skirts, lit upper planes, open notches.
 poly(p([[-10,-2],[-8,-5],[-8,-8],[-4,-9],[-1,-11],[2,-9],[5,-9],[6,-6],[9,-5],[10,-1],[8,2],[9,5],[5,6],[3,9],[-1,8],[-4,9],[-6,6],[-9,5],[-8,1]]),c[joined?1:0]);
 poly(p([[-9,-3],[-6,-7],[-2,-9],[2,-8],[6,-6],[8,-3],[7,1],[8,4],[4,5],[1,7],[-3,6],[-5,4],[-8,4],[-7,0]]),c[1+light]);
 poly(p([[-9,-4],[-7,-7],[-5,-7],[-4,-9],[-1,-8],[0,-6],[3,-6],[4,-3],[1,-3],[0,-1],[-2,-2],[-3,1],[-5,0],[-7,1],[-7,-2]]),c[2+light]);
 poly(p([[-8,-5],[-6,-8],[-3,-8],[-3,-6],[0,-7],[1,-5],[-2,-4],[-3,-2],[-5,-3],[-7,-2]]),c[3+light]);
 // Short hooked leaf planes, not random square confetti.
 for(const [x,y,k] of [[-6,-3,4],[-3,-6,5],[1,-4,4],[-2,0,3],[4,1,2],[-4,4,2]]){
  const a=p([[x,y]])[0];rect(a[0],a[1],Math.max(1,Math.round(r*.23)),1,c[Math.min(5,k)]);rect(a[0]-1,a[1]+1,2,1,c[Math.max(1,k-1)]);
 }
}

function withSurface(canvas,x,y,fn){const before=[ctx,ox,oy];ctx=canvas.getContext('2d');ctx.imageSmoothingEnabled=false;ox=x;oy=y;try{return fn();}finally{[ctx,ox,oy]=before;}}
function cacheDraw(key,x,y,paint){
 const cache=cacheDraw.frames||(cacheDraw.frames=new Map());let frame=cache.get(key);
 if(!frame){const dest=ctx.canvas,canvas=dest.ownerDocument?dest.ownerDocument.createElement('canvas'):new dest.constructor(384,288);canvas.width=384;canvas.height=288;withSurface(canvas,192-(x-y)*16,192-(x+y)*8,paint);frame=canvas;cache.set(key,frame);}
 const p=pt(x,y);ctx.drawImage(frame,p[0]-192,p[1]-192);
}

function rect(x,y,w,h,c){ctx.fillStyle=c;ctx.fillRect(Math.round(x),Math.round(y),Math.round(w),Math.round(h))}
function line(x0,y0,x1,y1,c){x0=Math.round(x0);x1=Math.round(x1);y0=Math.round(y0);y1=Math.round(y1);let dx=Math.abs(x1-x0),sx=x0<x1?1:-1,dy=-Math.abs(y1-y0),sy=y0<y1?1:-1,e=dx+dy;for(;;){rect(x0,y0,1,1,c);if(x0===x1&&y0===y1)break;let e2=2*e;if(e2>=dy){e+=dy;x0+=sx}if(e2<=dx){e+=dx;y0+=sy}}}
function poly(points,c){let p=points.map(a=>a.map(Math.round)),min=Math.min(...p.map(a=>a[1])),max=Math.max(...p.map(a=>a[1]));for(let y=min;y<=max;y++){let hits=[];for(let i=0,j=p.length-1;i<p.length;j=i++){let a=p[i],b=p[j];if((a[1]<=y&&b[1]>y)||(b[1]<=y&&a[1]>y))hits.push(a[0]+(y-a[1])/(b[1]-a[1])*(b[0]-a[0]));}hits.sort((a,b)=>a-b);for(let i=0;i<hits.length;i+=2)rect(Math.ceil(hits[i]),y,Math.floor(hits[i+1])-Math.ceil(hits[i])+1,1,c)}}
function outline(points,c){for(let i=0;i<points.length;i++)line(...points[i],...points[(i+1)%points.length],c)}
function plane(x,y,w,d,z,c,stroke){const p=[pt(x,y,z),pt(x+w,y,z),pt(x+w,y+d,z),pt(x,y+d,z)];poly(p,c);if(stroke)outline(p,stroke);return p}
function box(x,y,w,d,z,h,ramp,edge=true){const a=pt(x,y,z+h),b=pt(x+w,y,z+h),c=pt(x+w,y+d,z+h),d0=pt(x,y+d,z+h),b0=pt(x+w,y,z),c0=pt(x+w,y+d,z),d1=pt(x,y+d,z);poly([d0,c,c0,d1],ramp[2]);poly([b,c,c0,b0],ramp[1]);poly([a,b,c,d0],ramp[3]);if(edge){line(...d0,...c,ramp[3]);line(...b,...c,ramp[0]);line(...c,...c0,ramp[0]);line(...c0,...d1,ramp[0]);line(...c0,...b0,ramp[0])}}
function rnd(n){let s=Math.sin(n*127.1+311.7)*43758.5453;return s-Math.floor(s)}
function tile(x,y,kind='grass',variant=0){
 const r=P[kind]||P.grass;plane(x,y,1,1,0,r[2]);
 const key=((Math.floor(x)*3+Math.floor(y)*5+variant)%4+4)%4;
 if(kind==='pave'){
  // Slab joints obey the projection; one fine bright arris, one recessed joint.
  line(...pt(x,y),...pt(x+1,y),r[3]);line(...pt(x,y),...pt(x,y+1),r[3]);
  line(...pt(x+1,y),...pt(x+1,y+1),r[1]);line(...pt(x,y+1),...pt(x+1,y+1),r[1]);
  const split=key%2?.5:.625;line(...pt(x+split,y+.02),...pt(x+split,y+.98),r[1]);
  if(key===1){line(...pt(x+.08,y+.74),...pt(x+.22,y+.67),r[1]);rect(...pt(x+.2,y+.68),1,1,r[3]);}
  return;
 }
 const patches=[[[.13,.2],[.33,.16],[.46,.33],[.32,.42],[.16,.35]],[[.6,.55],[.77,.51],[.85,.69],[.73,.81],[.54,.75]],[[.28,.46],[.39,.3],[.6,.39],[.68,.57],[.48,.6]],[[.58,.14],[.83,.2],[.91,.32],[.69,.4],[.5,.3]]];
 poly(patches[key].map(([u,v])=>pt(x+u,y+v)),'rgba(40,72,34,.085)');
 const tufts=[[[.2,.64],[.65,.22],[.77,.78]],[[.23,.25],[.54,.7],[.87,.39]],[[.15,.46],[.59,.2],[.73,.72]],[[.34,.24],[.23,.79],[.84,.65]]];
 for(const [u,v] of tufts[key]){const [a,b]=pt(x+u,y+v);rect(a,b,2,1,r[1]);rect(a+1,b-1,1,1,r[3]);}
}

function tree(x,y,size=1,{z=0,receivers}={}){
 if(!tree.painting&&!receivers&&!refGroundShadow.receivers)return cacheDraw('tree:'+size+':'+z,x,y,()=>{tree.painting=true;try{tree(x,y,size,{z})}finally{tree.painting=false}});

 // A connected asymmetric crown underpainting avoids a stack of outlined leaf balls.

function shrub(x,y,w=1){

 }
}
